A mathematical modelling for integrated construction and production supply chain network design
Authors: Seyyed Saeed Helli, Hadi Mokhtari, Saeed Dehnavi
Published: 2025

ABSTRACT
Nowadays, the construction industry has become one of the most important and crucial sectors of an economy. With the introduction of the concept of the supply chain and the successful use of supply chain management in many production-based industries, it has also entered the construction industry in recent years. In a classical production-based supply chain, the flow of materials and outputs at the end of the chain consists of manufactured products. However, in the construction supply chain, the final output is a building or structure. In this paper, we propose integrating the construction supply chain with a production supply chain planning problem. The facility location decisions (opening and launching facilities), which are very traditional decisions in production supply chain problems, require significant construction activities. However, the construction supply chain concept is not considered in literature for such an important part of a production-based supply chain. When designing the topology of a production supply chain (numbers, capacities, geographic positions of plants and warehouses), a series of construction activities and projects begin to create the strategic structure for the supply chain. Subsequently, once operational, the production supply chain can start producing products to sell to customers. In this article, we will formulate such an integrated problem using a comprehensive mixed integer linear programming optimization model. The aim is designing an optimal structure for the supply chain with minimal construction and production costs by determining optimal amounts of building materials, raw materials, workers, equipment and machinery, transportation methods, and designs based on required construction size. A wide range of examples and test problems are presented, discussed, and compared in detail to investigate and illustrate the performance of proposed mathematical model.Supplementary InformationThe online version contains supplementary material available at 10.1038/s41598-025-21137-9.

Introduction

Supply chain is a network of facilities working together in different layers, along upstream to downstream, to provide high quality goods and services to customers. In traditional form of businesses, planning, manufacturing, transportation, marketing, and procurement operate independently. The supply chain, as a new management concept, integrates different members along businesses’ processes and helps businesses to increase efficiency and enhance responsiveness. Especially when we speak about complex and large industries, the use of supply chain concept becomes more necessary. A typical topology of a supply chain includes suppliers, production centers, distribution centers, retailers and customers layers. Supply chain management aims to plan and control the interaction and integration of different layers of the business, from the point of material procurement to the point of end-user consumption.

The construction has been one of the first human needs since beginning stages of human life history. This need has not only not diminished over time and years, but its importance has increased significantly during recent years, and it has found wider dimensions and more complexities. As a construction project grows, the amount of interactions with other sectors of the industry and, consequently, the complexity of the project increases. If in the past, people used construction in housing and as a shelter, today a large part of human needs are related to the construction industry and construction projects. Thousands of construction projects are being carried out in all parts of the world with various applications, such as road construction, commercial construction, dam and power plant construction projects. The large volume of activities and the magnitude of construction structures necessitate the use of supply chain concepts and techniques in the construction industry. Construction supply chain includes different parties, such as customers, building material suppliers, design companies, contractors, sub-contractors, and owners. Since construction industry can become complicated, the supply chain approach seems to be necessary to apply. In the late 1980 s, the construction industry saw the start of several supply chain-related initiatives1. With the expansion of the concept of supply chain and the use of supply chain management in manufacturing industries, supply chain management has also entered construction industry, and then researchers started to find methods to benefit from the supply chain management approach in construction industry. In terms of structure and function, the construction supply chain is characterized by the following elements1:

It is a converged supply chain, directing all materials to the construction site where the target (structure) is established from the input materials.Workers, equipment, machineries and materials all come together with a common goal and specific destination (construction site).

It is a converged supply chain, directing all materials to the construction site where the target (structure) is established from the input materials.

Construction supply chain is different from production supply chain because the nature of a construction project is heterogeneous with temporary project-based activities. The construction supply chain has inherent differences with the production supply chain, which are mentioned below:

In the classical production supply chain, the flow of materials and output at the end of the chain includes the manufactured product, but in the construction supply chain, the final output includes a structure or building.In construction supply chain, each customer requires a unique structure with its own special design and characteristics. While this is not the case in conventional production supply chains.The construction supply chain is project-base and then it’s temporary, but the classical production-based supply chain works continuously.A construction plant is built around a single product (construction site), but multiple products pass through the same production lines in manufacturing systems.

Following are some essential characteristics of the construction supply chains versus production supply chain as follows:

The product in the construction supply chain is mostly for a single customer or project.In construction, location, equipment and production methods change for each customer in some cases.Construction personnel have a high turnover rate during construction and between projects. That is, as seen in construction projects, different people may be employed at different times.Not all parts and materials can be stored at the workplace, in construction.No two construction projects are the same, and each project has special conditions.Construction supply chain aims to make a strategic decision about creating a structure or building, however the production supply chain is related to mid-term and operational decisions.

The rest of the paper is organized as follows: Sect. 2 reviews related literature on construction supply chain management. Section 3 elaborates proposed methodology in details. Discussions are presented in Sect. 4. Finally, Sect. 5 concludes the results.

Literature review

The application of supply chain in the construction industry is a relatively new topic. Xue et al.2 have presented a two-level planning model for collaborative planning in the construction supply chain. In this optimization problem, the goal has been to maximize profit where the variables of construction time and delivery time have been considered. The supply chain has three layers including: the main contractor, subcontractors and suppliers. Elmughrabi et al.3 have modeled and optimized the problem of joint planning of construction projects with the aim of minimizing the total cost of construction projects, including the cost of production, ordering, construction activities, transportation, inventory, fines and rewards for late or early completion of projects. The assumed supply chain structure includes the plants, warehouses and independent projects (contractors). Jian-hua and Wan4 have presented a two-level planning model for the time and cost balancing problem in the construction supply chain. In this article, the goal of optimization was to maximize the profit of the contract parties, considering the duration of the project and the cash flow per unit of time. Xu et al.5 presented an optimization model to minimize the cost of safety inventory and the cost of project implementation compression (material delivery delay costs) for repetitive projects with random material delivery delays. In this article, each activity has its own material supply chain. Koutsokosta and Katsavounis6 presented a multi-period, mixed and dynamic linear programming model to minimize the cost of a multi-site, multi-product construction supply chain. The goal is to minimize costs and inventory costs. The supply chain structure includes three layers of suppliers, warehouses (logistics centers) and project sites. Hsu et al.7 have presented an optimization model to minimize inventory costs and complete construction projects that include transportation, assembly, inventory, and uncertainty penalty costs. In this article, the supply chain structure includes the production centers, warehouses and construction sites. Mohammadnazari and Ghannadpour8 have presented a model for optimization in construction supply chain management with a focus on inventory optimization under uncertainty. The objective of the model is to minimize the total cost, including material purchase cost, inventory holding opportunity cost, warehouse cost, and ordering cost. The supply chain structure includes suppliers, warehouses and project storage locations. Feng et al.9, developed a model to solve the inherent conflicts between the decision makers of the construction supply chain. The goal of optimization has been to minimize total operating costs, minimize transportation costs, and maximize satisfaction variable, where the supply chain includes the production and the distribution departments. Golpîra10 presented the optimal integration of the facility location problem in the multi-project multi-supplier multi-source construction supply chain under vendor-managed inventory strategy. The goal was to minimize the total cost considering contract costs, inventory and pre-processing costs, transportation costs, facility location costs and project delivery late penalty. In this research, the structure of the supply chain includes the supplier, temporary warehouses, pre-processing locations and final site locations (project locations). They studied the amounts of resources transported from suppliers to temporary facilities and from temporary facilities to demand points. The decisions are related to the selection of the supplier and the allocation of temporary facilities to the candidate locations.

Some articles have reviewed the construction supply chain literature. Peter et al.11, have reviewed the fields of the supply chain in the construction industry in eight sections: logistics, purchasing, sustainability, customer relations, management of the construction environment, performance measurement, information and application of models in the construction supply chain. Behera et al.12 have evaluated the construction supply chain in the areas of conceptualization, planning, vendor selection, negotiation, sourcing, construction and delivery. Pushpamali et al.13 have studied the reverse logistics studies of the construction supply chain, especially in the context of the green construction supply chain. Kumar and Shoghli14, have reviewed the studies of the use of Internet of Things in the optimization of the building material supply chain. Chen et al.15have investigated and studied coordination factors in construction supply chain processes. Cutting-Decelle et al.16, studied and investigated the types of communication in supply chains, from production to construction. O’Brien et al.17 examined the different dimensions of coordination in modeling the construction supply chain from an economic point of view. In this article, the supply chain structure including owner, design company, contractor, site location, subcontractor and supplier was studied. Aloini et al.18 have reviewed operational risks in the construction supply chain. This study has investigated the development of the application of supply chain management in the construction industry and the risk factors affecting the implementation of the principles of supply chain management in this industry as well. In addition, some recent researches considered the mathematical programming and optimization approach for addressing different aspects of construction supply chain planning problems in literature19–21.

As it can be seen from above literature review, formal integration into a unified framework is largely absent in the literature. In traditional production supply chain problems, there are mainly two different decision categories, which are: (i) facility location problem and (ii) flow allocation problem. In facility location problems, the location of facilities, such as plants and warehouses, is determined to be opened, while the main decisions in allocation problems are such as: capacity and transportation planning, operations scheduling, staff planning, etc. The facility location (opening and launch of facilities) is a very traditional decisions in production supply chain problems, and it needs construction activities, however the construction supply chain is not considered in literature for such an important part of production-based supply chain. When the topology of a production supply chain is designed (numbers, capacities, geographic positions of plants and warehouses), then a bunch of construction activities and projects commence to create strategic structure of supply chain. Afterwards, the production supply chain is operational and can start to produce products and sell to customers. In this article, we will study and discuss such an integrated problem which investigates simultaneous consideration of construction and production supply chains.

Methodology

Problem description

As discussed before, an integrated construction and production supply chain network design problem is considered in this article. In classical supply chain problems, it is assumed that all the buildings and sites are constructed in advance and then the problem is just to decide about flow of materials, productions and purchases within existing supply chain. However, in this article we aim to consider a supply chain for construction of buildings and structures for both production and distribution centers. The construction supply chain network considered in this study is a single-interval network consisting of construction sites, design companies, contractors, and suppliers of building materials. In the assumed network, construction operations are carried out at selected construction sites with pre-specified minimum and maximum number of locations. The minimum construction area for each selected site is predetermined, as well as the maximum capacity of each construction site. Each selected construction site has only one contractor. The land of different construction sites has different prices, which are taken into account in the problem. Design companies are responsible for designing selected construction sites, preparing and providing design documents and drawings. It should be note that the design companies are outside of the factory and is treated as an independent entity such as consulting firms. The problem ensures that the designed area of each construction site equals the actual area to be constructed there. The contractors execute the constructions and are responsible for coordinating and providing worker, equipment, machinery, receiving materials from suppliers. The amount of the construction area is proportional to the level of production and distribution that is needed inside the production and retailer sites. The problem guarantees that each construction site has at most one contractor. The minimum and maximum number of contractors is also predetermined. The suppliers of building materials are responsible for providing the necessary materials for projects with the maximum number of suppliers, their capacities all predetermined in advance.

After that construction (of production and distribution center sites) are carried out and finished, a production-based supply chain is also considered in order to produce a commercial product and sell into customers (from supply to sell layers). The production-based supply chain considered in this study is a single-loop network with direct flow consisting of four layers: suppliers, production centers, distribution centers, and customers. In the first layer of the assumed network, raw material suppliers are placed, with their capacities predetermined. We should optimally determine, at this layer, that how many materials should be purchased from each supplier according to their price and transportation costs and how many of purchased materials should be transported into each production center. The production centers are responsible for producing products, with the capacity of each production center pre-determined. The distribution centers in the third layer have their predetermined capacity for holding items as well. The customers in the fourth layer have their specific demands. The assumed network is multi-product, aiming to meet customer demand for products at minimal cost. Raw materials are first purchased from contracted suppliers and then sent to operational production centers. Products are manufactured at these centers and sent to distribution centers. Finally, distribution centers provide products to customers with specific predefined demands. Additionally, there is no transfer of products between two identical facilities in this model; for example, product transfer does not occur between distribution centers. The proposed problem has been considered primarily for the employer (project owner), who is interested in investment and design of the supply chain. While design companies, suppliers, and retailers may benefit from the results indirectly, they are not the main audience of the model. The main goal is to support the project owner purpose in establishing an integrated and cost-effective construction–production supply chain.

Figure 1 represents the conceptual diagram of the model.

Fig. 1The conceptual diagram of model.

The conceptual diagram of model.

In the following, we will present the suggested mathematical programming optimization model by introducing the notations, objective function (total cost), and technical constraints.

Objective function (total cost)

The total transportation cost between supplier and potential production centers can be calculated by multiplying the cost of transporting each unit of raw material by the amount of raw material transported expresses. So total cost of transporting raw material from suppliers to production centers can be formulated as follows:1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:TRSP=\sum\:_{i}\sum\:_{j}\sum\:_{m}{CA}_{ij}^{m}{PA}_{ij}^{m}$$\end{document}

The total transportation cost between production center and distribution center results from the product of the cost of transporting each unit of product and the quantity of transported product. So total cost of transporting products from production centers to distribution centers can be given as follows.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:TRPD=\sum\:_{j}\sum\:_{k}\sum\:_{m}{CB}_{jk}^{m}{PB}_{jk}^{m}$$\end{document}

Using the cost of transporting each unit of product and the amount of product transported obtain the total cost of transportation between the distribution center and the customer. So total cost of transporting the products from the distribution centers to the customers can be expressed as:3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:TRDC=\sum\:_{k}\sum\:_{l}\sum\:_{m}{CC}_{kl}^{m}{PC}_{kl}^{m}$$\end{document}

When the production center is launched, a binary variable assigns a value of one to it, resulting in the cost of setup being the product of this variable and the cost parameter. So total cost of setting up the production lines for production centers can be stated as:4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:TSP=\sum\:_{j}{f}_{j}{Y}_{j}$$\end{document}

In the case of a contract with the supplier, a binary variable assigns a value of one to it, resulting in the product of the variable and the cost parameter representing the contract cost with the supplier. Then total cost of the contracts with suppliers is formulated as follows.5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:TCS=\sum\:_{i}{gs}_{i}{Z}_{i}$$\end{document}

The total cost of producing products at production centers is:6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:TPP=\sum\:_{i}\sum\:_{j}\sum\:_{m}{S}_{j}^{m}{PA}_{ij}^{m}$$\end{document}

The total cost of transportation between the supplier of building materials and the contractor’s warehouse results from product of the cost of transporting each unit of product and the quantity of product transported. It can be calculated as follows:7\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:TMSC=\sum\:_{p}\sum\:_{c}\sum\:_{n}{TC}_{pc}^{n}{XS}_{pc}^{n}$$\end{document}

To calculate the total cost of transportation between the contractor’s warehouse and the potential production center, the cost of transporting each unit of building materials is multiplied by the amount of building materials transported as follows:8\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:TMCP=\sum\:_{c}\sum\:_{j}\sum\:_{n}{TD}_{cj}^{n}{XB}_{cj}{DM}_{cj}^{n}$$\end{document}

Using the cost of transporting each worker and the transported workforce obtain the cost of transporting workers by contractors to the potential production center. Therefore, total cost of transporting workers from contractors to potential production center can be formulated as:9\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:TWCP=\sum\:_{c}\sum\:_{j}\sum\:_{h}{TE}_{cj}^{h}{XB}_{cj}{DH}_{cj}^{h}$$\end{document}

In the case of a contract with a building materials supplier, a binary variable selects either zero or one, resulting in the cost of the contract with suppliers being shown by multiplying this variable by the cost parameter. So total cost of the contract with suppliers for building materials and supplies is:10\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:TCSM=\sum\:_{p}{{R}_{p}ES}_{p}$$\end{document}

In the event of a contract with the contractor, a binary variable selects a value of one, resulting in the product of the variable and the cost parameter representing the cost of the contract with the contractor. Hence, total cost of the contract with contractors can be represented as:11\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:TCC=\sum\:_{c}{{V}_{c}EP}_{c}$$\end{document}

When using the contractor’s warehouse, a variable zero-one is chosen, resulting in the product of the variable and the cost parameter representing the fixed operational cost. The total fixed cost of contractors’ warehouses is:12\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:TCW=\sum\:_{c}{{U}_{c}EP}_{c}$$\end{document}

When purchasing the desired land, a binary variable selects either 0 or 1, resulting in the cost of the purchase contract being the product of this variable and the cost parameter. So, the total cost of contracts for purchasing lands is:13\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:TCLC=\sum\:_{j}{{Q}_{j}Y}_{j}$$\end{document}

The output of the multiplying the variable by cost parameters is the cost of purchasing land for construction sites. So, the total cost of purchasing lands is:14\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:TCLP=\sum\:_{c}\sum\:_{j}{G}_{j}{XB}_{cj}$$\end{document}

The total cost for designs can be modeled as:15\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:TCD=\sum\:_{d}\sum\:_{j}{T}_{dj}{XM}_{dj}$$\end{document}

The total cost of purchasing building materials is computed as:16\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:TPM=\sum\:_{p}\sum\:_{c}\sum\:_{n}{MC}_{pc}^{n}{XS}_{pc}^{n}$$\end{document}

The total cost of storage of building materials and supplies is calculated as:17\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:TSM=\sum\:_{p}\sum\:_{c}\sum\:_{n}{MP}_{c}^{n}{XS}_{pc}^{n}$$\end{document}

By multiplication of the cost of hiring each worker per day by the number of workers hired represents the cost of hiring contractor workers at the construction site. So, the total cost of employing workers can be stated as:18\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:TEW=\sum\:_{c}\sum\:_{j}\sum\:_{h}{MN}_{c}^{h}{XB}_{cj}{DH}_{cj}^{h}$$\end{document}

The cost of employing equipment and machinery by contractors at the construction site results from the product of the cost of employing each unit of equipment and machinery per day by the number of days the equipment and machinery are employed. Hence, total cost of providing all equipment and machinery is:19\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:TPEM=\sum\:_{c}\sum\:_{j}\sum\:_{e}{MO}_{c}^{e}{XB}_{cj}{DE}_{cj}^{e}$$\end{document}

Using the cost of transporting each unit of product and the quantity of product transported obtain the total cost of transportation between the building materials supplier and the contractor’s warehouse. So, total cost of transporting building materials from the suppliers to the contractors is:20\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:TCSC=\sum\:_{a}\sum\:_{b}\sum\:_{g}{TCDC}_{ab}^{g}{XSDC}_{ab}^{g}$$\end{document}

The multiplication of the cost of transporting each unit of building materials in the quantity transported represents the total cost of transportation between the contractor’s warehouse and the construction site. Hence, total cost of transporting building materials and supplies from the contractor’s warehouses to the construction sites is:21\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:TCCC=\sum\:_{b}\sum\:_{k}\sum\:_{g}{TDDC}_{bk}^{g}{XBDC}_{bk}{DMDC}_{bk}^{g}$$\end{document}

Multiplying the cost of transporting each worker by the transported workforce expresses the cost of transporting workers from contractors to the construction site as follows. Then, the total cost of transporting workers can be computed by:22\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:TTW=\sum\:_{b}\sum\:_{k}\sum\:_{w}{TEDC}_{bk}^{w}{XBDC}_{bk}{DHDC}_{bk}^{w}$$\end{document}

In the case of a contract with the supplier of building materials and supplies, a variable that takes on values of zero and one is chosen, and as a result, the product of the variable and the cost parameter represents the cost of the contract with suppliers. The total cost of the contracts with the suppliers is:23\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:TCSS=\sum\:_{a}{{RDC}_{a}ESDC}_{a}$$\end{document}

In the case of a contract with the contractor, a variable that takes on values of zero and one is chosen, and as a result, multiplying this variable by the cost parameter shows the cost of the contract with the contractor. So, the total cost of the contracts with the contractors is:24\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:TCCS=\sum\:_{b}{{VDC}_{b}EPDC}_{b}$$\end{document}

When using the contractor’s warehouse, a variable assigns a value of one to it, resulting in the fixed operational cost being shown as the product of the variable and the cost parameter. So, total fixed cost of using the contractors’ warehouses is:25\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:TFC=\sum\:_{b}{{UDC}_{b}EPDC}_{b}$$\end{document}

When purchasing the desired land, a variable takes on the value of one, and the product of this variable with the cost parameter represents the land purchase contract cost. Then, total cost of purchasing land for construction projects is:26\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:TFLC=\sum\:_{k}{{QDC}_{k}YDC}_{k}$$\end{document}

The product of the variable and cost parameters represents the cost of purchasing land for construction sites. The total cost of purchasing lands for construction sites is formulated as:27\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:TCPL=\sum\:_{b}\sum\:_{k}{GDC}_{k}{XBDC}_{bk}$$\end{document}

Multiplying the variable by cost parameters indicates the design fee for construction site design companies. Therefore, total cost of designing construction sites can be computed as:28\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:TCDC=\sum\:_{f}\sum\:_{k}{TDC}_{fk}{XMDC}_{fk}$$\end{document}

The cost of purchasing building materials from the supplier by the contractor is outcome of multiplying the product of the variable by the cost parameters. Hence, total cost of purchasing building materials is:29\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:TCPM=\sum\:_{a}\sum\:_{b}\sum\:_{g}{MCDC}_{ab}^{g}{XSDC}_{ab}^{g}$$\end{document}

The product of this variable with cost parameters indicates the cost of storing building materials and supplies in the warehouse. The total cost of storing/holding building materials and supplies in the contractor’s warehouses is:30\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:TCHM=\sum\:_{a}\sum\:_{b}\sum\:_{g}{MPDC}_{b}^{g}{XSDC}_{ab}^{g}$$\end{document}

By multiplication of the cost of hiring each worker per day by the number of workers hired represents the cost of hiring worker for contractors at the construction site. So, the total cost of workers can be stated as:31\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:TCWR=\sum\:_{b}\sum\:_{k}\sum\:_{w}{MNDC}_{b}^{w}{XBDC}_{bk}{DHDC}_{bk}^{w}$$\end{document}

To calculate the cost of employing equipment and machinery for contractors at the construction site, the cost of employing each equipment and machinery per day is multiplied by the number of days they are employed. Therefore, the total cost of equipment and machinery is:32\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:TCEM=\sum\:_{b}\sum\:_{k}\sum\:_{v}{MODC}_{b}^{v}{XBDC}_{bk}{DEDC}_{bk}^{v}$$\end{document}

The total objective function of the problem is to minimize the total costs of the supply chain network \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:TC$$\end{document}, i.e., sum of all of the above costs, which is presented below.33\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}\text{min}TC&=\sum\:_{i}\sum\:_{j}\sum\:_{m}{CA}_{ij}^{m}{PA}_{ij}^{m}+\sum\:_{j}\sum\:_{k}\sum\:_{m}{CB}_{jk}^{m}{PB}_{jk}^{m}\\&+\sum\:_{k}\sum\:_{l}\sum\:_{m}{CC}_{kl}^{m}{PC}_{kl}^{m}\:+\sum\:_{j}{f}_{j}{Y}_{j}+\sum\:_{i}{gs}_{i}{Z}_{i}\\&+\sum\:_{i}\sum\:_{j}\sum\:_{m}{S}_{j}^{m}{PA}_{ij}^{m}\:+\sum\:_{p}\sum\:_{c}\sum\:_{n}{TC}_{pc}^{n}{XS}_{pc}^{n}\\&+\sum\:_{c}\sum\:_{j}\sum\:_{n}{TD}_{cj}^{n}{XB}_{cj}{DM}_{cj}^{n}+\sum\:_{c}\sum\:_{j}\sum\:_{h}{TE}_{cj}^{h}{XB}_{cj}{DH}_{cj}^{h}\\&+\sum\:_{p}{{R}_{p}ES}_{p}+\sum\:_{c}{{V}_{c}EP}_{c}+\sum\:_{c}{{U}_{c}EP}_{c}+\sum\:_{j}{{Q}_{j}Y}_{j}+\sum\:_{c}\sum\:_{j}{G}_{j}{XB}_{cj}+\sum\:_{d}\sum\:_{j}{T}_{dj}{XM}_{dj}\\&+\sum\:_{p}\sum\:_{c}\sum\:_{n}{MC}_{pc}^{n}{XS}_{pc}^{n}+\sum\:_{p}\sum\:_{c}\sum\:_{n}{MP}_{c}^{n}{XS}_{pc}^{n}\\&+\sum\:_{c}\sum\:_{j}\sum\:_{h}{MN}_{c}^{h}{XB}_{cj}{DH}_{cj}^{h}+\sum\:_{c}\sum\:_{j}\sum\:_{e}{MO}_{c}^{e}{XB}_{cj}{DE}_{cj}^{e}+\sum\:_{a}\sum\:_{b}\sum\:_{g}{TCDC}_{ab}^{g}{XSDC}_{ab}^{g}\\&+\sum\:_{b}\sum\:_{k}\sum\:_{g}{TDDC}_{bk}^{g}{XBDC}_{bk}{DMDC}_{bk}^{g}+\sum\:_{b}\sum\:_{k}\sum\:_{w}{TEDC}_{bk}^{w}{XBDC}_{bk}{DHDC}_{bk}^{w}\\&+\sum\:_{a}{{RDC}_{a}ESDC}_{a}+\sum\:_{b}{{VDC}_{b}EPDC}_{b}+\sum\:_{b}{{UDC}_{b}EPDC}_{b}+\sum\:_{k}{{QDC}_{k}YDC}_{k}\\&+\sum\:_{b}\sum\:_{k}{GDC}_{k}{XBDC}_{bk}+\sum\:_{f}\sum\:_{k}{TDC}_{fk}{XMDC}_{fk}\\&+\sum\:_{a}\sum\:_{b}\sum\:_{g}{MCDC}_{ab}^{g}{XSDC}_{ab}^{g}+\sum\:_{a}\sum\:_{b}\sum\:_{g}{MPDC}_{b}^{g}{XSDC}_{ab}^{g}\\&+\sum\:_{b}\sum\:_{k}\sum\:_{w}{MNDC}_{b}^{w}{XBDC}_{bk}{DHDC}_{bk}^{w}+\sum\:_{b}\sum\:_{k}\sum\:_{v}{MODC}_{b}^{v}{XBDC}_{bk}{DEDC}_{bk}^{v}\end{aligned}$$\end{document}

Technical constraints

Equation (34) ensures that customer demand for product \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:m$$\end{document} is met. This means the quantity of product \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:m$$\end{document} transported from all distribution centers to customer point \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:l$$\end{document}, represented by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\sum\:_{k}{PC}_{kl}^{m}$$\end{document}, must be greater than or equal to the demand for each type of product \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:m$$\end{document} at customer point \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:l$$\end{document}, i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} Constraint (44) indicates that raw materials for products are only sent from suppliers to potential production centers, and if a production center is not open, no product is sent to that production center. Constraint (50) indicates that products from production centers are only sent to selected distribution centers, and if a distribution center is not selected and operated, no products are sent to that distribution center. Constraint (77) relates to the proportionality of the construction area in each distribution center’s construction site with the construction needs of that distribution center based on the number of products needed by that distribution center. Constraint (79) indicates that construction only takes place in construction sites where the land has been purchased and a land purchase contract has been established. If the land purchase contract is not established, construction does not take place there. Constraint (80) is complementary to Constraint (79) and ensures that if land is purchased, construction must be carried out there.79\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\sum\:_{b}{XBDC}_{bk}\le\:\rho\:*{YDC}_{k}\:\:\:\forall\:k$$\end{document}80\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\sum\:_{b}{XBDC}_{bk}\ge\:{YDC}_{k}\:\:\:\forall\:k$$\end{document}

Constraint (81) indicates that construction, procurement of building materials, storage of materials, supply of worker, equipment, and machinery are carried out only by selected contractors with whom contracts have been established. If a contractor is not selected, construction will not be carried out by that contractor. Constraint (82) complements Constraint (81) and ensures that if a contractor is selected, construction must be done by that contractor.81\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\sum\:_{k}{XBDC}_{bk}\le\:\rho\:*{EPDC}_{b}\:\:\:\forall\:b$$\end{document}82\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\sum\:_{k}{XBDC}_{bk}\ge\:{EPDC}_{b}\:\:\:\forall\:b$$\end{document}

Constraint (83) indicates that the purchase and receipt of building materials are only done by contractors who have been selected and contracted with. If a contractor is not selected, the purchase and receipt of building materials will not be carried out by that contractor. Constraint (84) complements Constraint (83) and ensures that if a contractor is selected, they must definitely carry out the purchase and receipt of building materials.83\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\sum\:_{a}\sum\:_{g}{XSDC}_{ab}^{g}\le\:\rho\:*{EPDC}_{b}\:\:\:\forall\:b$$\end{document}84\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\sum\:_{a}\sum\:_{g}{XSDC}_{ab}^{g}\ge\:{EPDC}_{b}\:\:\:\forall\:b$$\end{document}

Constraint (85) indicates that the sale and delivery of building materials is only done by suppliers who have been selected and contracted with. If no supplier is selected, the sale and delivery of building materials will not be carried out by that supplier. Constraint (86) complements Constraint (85) and ensures that if a supplier is selected, sales and deliveries must be done by that supplier.85\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\sum\:_{b}\sum\:_{g}{XSDC}_{ab}^{g}\le\:\rho\:*{ESDC}_{a}\:\:\:\forall\:a$$\end{document}86\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\sum\:_{b}\sum\:_{g}{XSDC}_{ab}^{g}\ge\:{ESDC}_{a}\:\:\:\forall\:a$$\end{document}

Constraint (87) is an equilibrium constraint that ensures that the total demand for building materials and supplies (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:g$$\end{document}) of construction site projects by contractor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:b$$\end{document} is equal to the total building materials and supplies (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:g$$\end{document}) sent from all building material suppliers to the contractor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:c$$\end{document}‘s project warehouse. (For construction of distribution centers).87\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\sum\:_{k}{XBDC}_{bk}{DMDC}_{bk}^{g}=\sum\:_{a}{XSDC}_{ab}^{g}\:\:\:\forall\:g,b$$\end{document}

The storage capacity constraint of the contractor’s warehouse is indicated by relation (88). If the project contractor’s warehouse \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:b$$\end{document} is open, the amount of product \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:k$$\end{document} stored in it cannot exceed the capacity of the project contractor’s warehouse.88\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\sum\:_{k}\sum\:_{g}{XBDC}_{bk}{DMDC}_{bk}^{g}\le\:{WQDC}_{b}\:\:\:\forall\:b$$\end{document}

Discussion

In order to illustrate the performance of the proposed mathematical programming approach for the integrated construction and production supply chain planning problem, we perform three different analyses in this section: (i) At first, an illustrative example is considered and analyzed in details in order to illustrate the optimal structure and flows inside the integrated supply chain. In this example, we illustrate the optimal structure of obtained supply chain graphically (by its topology), (ii) Moreover, we carry out a comprehensive cost analysis by developing various test problems in second part. For better analysis and categorization, we classified the test problems into three different sizes, i.e., small (S), medium (M) and large (L) and discuss each one separately and collectively. For each category, four instances with different dimensions (totally 12 different instances) are considered and solved, and the performance of the proposed approaches is recorded and analyzed according to the objective function (total cost and each type of costs separately) and CPU time, (iii) Finally, a sensitivity analysis is performed to reach some managerial insights. All the runs are implemented by using GAMS on a 64-bit, Cori7, 8 RAM memory PC.

The proposed model is formulated as a linear programming problem, and its global optimum solution can be obtained with certainty. In linear programming, any local optimum is also a global optimum due to convex structure of such problems. Hence, by using this feature, we can confidently conclude that the obtained solution corresponds to the optimal solution for the given problem. When the formulation is linear, GAMS uses reliable solvers that explores the entire solution space and ensures convergence to the optimal solution and guarantees 100% accuracy. As a result, all following numerical results presented in the subsequent sections represent the exact solutions of the model rather than approximations. This ensures the credibility of our analysis and supports the validity of the computational experiments.

Illustrative example

In this section, we will present and analyze an illustrative example in order to illustrate more details of the proposed integrated construction and production supply chain planning problem. In this problem, the goal is to design and build an industrial production-based supply chain with the ability to produce three types of products with the demands from three different customers (retailers). In this supply chain, it is possible to buy raw materials from two suppliers. A production center (factory) must be built to produce products. The factory (after built) buys raw materials from suppliers and then produce products through its production processes. The products produced by the factory are transported to the warehouses of the distribution centers (wholesalers). Before that, the warehouses must be constructed from two candidate locations. At final stage of supply chain, the products are transported and delivered from warehouses to customers. The demands for product 1 are (448, 587, 460), for product 2 are (449, 476, 425), and for product 3 are (426, 557, 550) from customers 1, 2, and 3 respectively. For the construction of factories and distribution centers, the construction area is estimated and based on that, some contracts are signed with the design companies and construction contractors. It is also assumed that three types of equipment and machinery are required. For this purpose, two design companies and two contractor companies are available. Also, contractors can buy the building materials they need from two suppliers of building materials. It is also assumed that three categories of building materials are needed. Moreover, two types of workers, i.e., civil engineer and operator, are needed for the construction of both production and distribution centers. The summary characteristics of the problem is as follows:

Number of available contractors: 2.Number of available design companies: 2.Number of available raw material suppliers: 2.Number of available building material suppliers (for factory construction): 2.Number of available building material suppliers (for wholesaler construction): 2.Number of products (which there is demand for them): 3.Number of customers (retailers): 3.

Number of available contractors: 2.

Number of available contractors: 2.

Number of available design companies: 2.

Number of available design companies: 2.

Number of available raw material suppliers: 2.

Number of available raw material suppliers: 2.

Number of available building material suppliers (for factory construction): 2.

Number of available building material suppliers (for factory construction): 2.

Number of available building material suppliers (for wholesaler construction): 2.

Number of available building material suppliers (for wholesaler construction): 2.

Number of products (which there is demand for them): 3.

Number of products (which there is demand for them): 3.

Number of customers (retailers): 3.

Number of customers (retailers): 3.

Figure 2 shows a typical structure of production supply chain we are going to build in this problem. There are four layers (echelons) including: raw material suppliers, production centers (factory), distribution centers (wholesalers), and customers (retailers) in this supply chain. The suppliers and retailers are now available (everywhere in the market); however, we assume that a craftsman or entrepreneur wants to enter into market and needs to construct a factory and some possible distribution center(s) in order to build his/her business.

Fig. 2The structure of production supply chain (to be built).

The structure of production supply chain (to be built).

In order to design this supply chain optimally, we should decide about the following topics and questions:

Which suppliers should be selected and contracted with?Which contractors should be selected and contracted with?Which design companies should be selected and contracted with?How much transportation (materials, products, equipment, worker) should flow on construction and production supply chain networks?How much area should be constructed by each contractor?How much area should be designed by each design company?How much demand of customers (products) should be satisfied by each distribution centers (wholesaler)?How much demand of distribution centers (products) should be satisfied by each production center (factory)?

How much demand of distribution centers (products) should be satisfied by each production center (factory)?

After solving the model, the optimal structure of the supply chain was obtained in such a way that one factory and one wholesaler should be constructed. It is also optimal for the factory to purchase raw materials for production process from one supplier out of two available raw material suppliers. And after producing the products, factory transports the products to the wholesale warehouses for distribution to the customers. The optimal number of transported products is 1495, 1350 and 1533 for three types of products respectively. In order to construct the factory and the distribution center, contracts are signed with a design company (out of two available design companies) and with a contractor company (out of two available contractors). The construction area of ​​the factory is 4000 square meters and the construction area of ​​the distribution center is 2000 square meters. The transported materials from supplier to contractor for the construction of factory is (12000, 8000, 4000) and for the construction of retailer site is (2000, 6000, 6000), both for material types 1, 2 and 3 respectively. According to the output of model, 12,000 and 4000 man-hours civil engineer and operator are needed for construction of the production center. Additionally, 4000 and 2000 man-hours civil engineer and operator are needed for construction of the distribution center. Also, 16,000, 4000, 8000 machine-hours for machine type 1, 2 and 3 are optimal for the construction of the production center. Besides, 8000, 6000, 2000 machine-hours for machine type 1, 2 and 3 are optimal for the construction of the distribution center. The optimal total cost is calculated 2.1686E + 7$.

Figure 3 shows a graphical representation of optimal structure of this supply chain. It’s the optimal structure with the lowest possible cost which completely satisfies all the demands and needs for the designs, constructions, and productions.

Comparative analysis

In this section, a comprehensive analysis and comparisons are performed by using a wide collection of test problems. First, we define the dimensions for three categories of small, medium, and large problems. Table 3 illustrates the indices for each category of instances. The third column of table shows the dimensions for test problems, for example, I denotes the number of raw material suppliers, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:J$$\end{document} implies number of potential production centers, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:K$$\end{document} represents the number of potential distribution centers, and so on. As a further information, we calculated the number of variables and constraints of test problems and presented them in last two columns of the table, as well. In order to further illustrate the increasing dimension and complexity of test problems, Fig. 4 shows trend of number of variables and constraints for all test problems. A closer look at the chart reveals that, there are obvious differences among dimensions of small, medium and large problems in the figure. The magnitude of last four data on figure (Large problems: L1-L4) are superior than middle four data (Medium problems: M1-M4), and again, this is obvious for middle four data (Medium problems: M1-M4) than first four data (Small problems: S1-S4).

The dimensions of test problems.

Fig. 4The increasing trend of dimensions for test problems.

The increasing trend of dimensions for test problems.

In order to examine the results more accurately and in more detail, the optimal solution for all types of costs was presented separately in Table 4. Also, the percentage of each type of cost from the total amount of costs is also calculated and shown in Table 5. The increasing trend of costs is depicted separately for small, medium and large problems by Fig. 5. As can be seen, there is an almost upward trend in all types of expenses for all problems. The supply chains with larger dimensions will cost more, which is also true in reality. Also, Tables 6 and 7, and Figs. 6 and 7, and 8 show an illustration of all types of costs and their share in the total cost (in percent) for three categories of small, medium, and large problems. Finally, Table 8 shows the total costs as well as the execution (CPU) time of the program for different types of examples, and based on that, the charts 9 and 10 show the trend of these two, as well. The smallest supply chain, which is related to the S1 problem, needs the lowest total cost, which is 28,535,000$, and the largest chain, which is related to the L4 problem, needs the highest total cost, which is 14,070,000$, for the design, construction and planning of supply chain infrastructures and operations. Figures 9 and 10 depicts trends of small, medium and large problems for total cost and CPU time graphically. As a further analysis, we can figure out that the most expensive parts of supply chain are related to TCPL (cost of purchasing land for production centers construction), TCLP (cost of purchasing land for distribution centers construction), TPEM (total cost of equipment and machinery) and TEW (total cost of workers), respectively. Moreover, the least expensive parts of supply chain are related to TRSP (total cost of transporting raw material from supplier to production center), TRPD (total cost of transporting raw material from production center to wholesaler), TRDC (total cost of transporting raw material from production center to retailers) and TPP (total cost of producing products at production center), respectively. Note that these costs are itself large but relative to the other sectors cost of supply chain, they include relatively small percentage shares.

Sensitivity analysis

Sensitivity analysis is a method used to identify how variations in input values (parameters) for a given variable will impact the results of a mathematical model. In here, sensitivity analysis is conducted on four categories of parameters: transfer costs, fixed costs, demands, and capacities, which the following:

Fixed costs:The fixed cost of the contract with suppliers of building materials.The fixed cost of contracts with contractors.The fixed cost of using contractors’ warehouse.The fixed cost of land purchase contract for construction of production center.The fixed cost of land purchase contract for construction of distribution center.Transportation costs:The cost of transporting the building materials from suppliers to the contractor’s warehouse.The cost of transporting the building materials from the contractor’s warehouse to the production center.The cost of transporting the building materials from the contractor’s warehouse to the distribution center.The cost of transporting the contractors’ labor per person per day to the production center.The cost of transporting the contractors’ labor per person per day to the distribution center.Demands:The demand for building materials in construction sites by contractors.The labor demand in construction sites by contractors.The demand for equipment and machinery in construction sites by contractors.Land costs:The unit cost of land purchasing for production center.The unit cost of land purchasing for distribution center.

Each parameter is changed between − 50% and + 50%. Table 9 displays the sensitivity analysis performed on above various considered parameters. Additionally, the cost function’s evolution due to fluctuations in four categories of parameters is depicted in Fig. 11. It is evident that the optimal cost increases directly across all four categories of parameters (fixed and transportation costs, demands, and land costs). Moreover, to assess the magnitude of cost sensitivity to these parameters, the approximate slope of the lines (in Fig. 11) can be estimated as follows:102\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:\:AS=\frac{Total\:Cost\:\left(50\%\right)-Total\:Cost\:(-50\%)}{100}$$\end{document}

This relationship represents the approximate slope of the line, obtained from changes in the objective function for two values, + 50% and − 50% (vertical axis), divided by the difference of + 50% and − 50% (horizontal axis), which linearly approximates the sensitivity of the cost function to the parameters. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:AS$$\end{document} means the amount of changes in the cost function per 1% change in the parameters. After calculation, the sensitivity value for the fixed costs parameter is 1.11E + 03, for transportation costs is 9.88E + 03, for demands is 1.32E + 05, and for land costs is 7.28E + 04, indicating the highest sensitivity for demands and the lowest sensitivity for fixed costs.

As can be seen from Table 9, the problem has become infeasible for some scenarios, which means that some constraints of the illustrative example are violated. It means that some construction resources or capacities are not sufficient to meet the demand of customers and supply chain faced shortages. Specifically, warehouse capacity (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\:{WQ}_{c}$$\end{document}) causes solution infeasibility which can be resolved by 8% and 15% increase for the + 40% and + 50% scenarios respectively.

Limitations

As some limitations for this research, the following suggestions can be considered:

Model Assumptions: The current formulation relies on deterministic parameters and does not explicitly account for uncertainties in demand, costs, lead times, or other stochastic factors. This assumption simplifies the modeling and solution process but may limit applicability in highly volatile real-world environments.Scalability and Computational Complexity: The model has been tested on small, medium, and relatively large instances, with computational times ranging from 0.016 to 0.343 s. These results confirm that the solution procedure is efficient and computationally tractable for the considered problem sizes. Nevertheless, in the case of very large-scale and highly complex real-world networks, additional research on advanced solution techniques (e.g., decomposition methods, heuristics, or metaheuristics) could further ensure scalability and efficiency.Validation: The model has been validated through illustrative examples, comparative assessments, and sensitivity analyses, which provide meaningful insights into its robustness and applicability. Nevertheless, additional validation through real-world case studies or simulation-based experiments would further enhance the practical relevance and external validity of the approach as future research.

Model Assumptions: The current formulation relies on deterministic parameters and does not explicitly account for uncertainties in demand, costs, lead times, or other stochastic factors. This assumption simplifies the modeling and solution process but may limit applicability in highly volatile real-world environments.

Model Assumptions: The current formulation relies on deterministic parameters and does not explicitly account for uncertainties in demand, costs, lead times, or other stochastic factors. This assumption simplifies the modeling and solution process but may limit applicability in highly volatile real-world environments.

Scalability and Computational Complexity: The model has been tested on small, medium, and relatively large instances, with computational times ranging from 0.016 to 0.343 s. These results confirm that the solution procedure is efficient and computationally tractable for the considered problem sizes. Nevertheless, in the case of very large-scale and highly complex real-world networks, additional research on advanced solution techniques (e.g., decomposition methods, heuristics, or metaheuristics) could further ensure scalability and efficiency.

Scalability and Computational Complexity: The model has been tested on small, medium, and relatively large instances, with computational times ranging from 0.016 to 0.343 s. These results confirm that the solution procedure is efficient and computationally tractable for the considered problem sizes. Nevertheless, in the case of very large-scale and highly complex real-world networks, additional research on advanced solution techniques (e.g., decomposition methods, heuristics, or metaheuristics) could further ensure scalability and efficiency.

Validation: The model has been validated through illustrative examples, comparative assessments, and sensitivity analyses, which provide meaningful insights into its robustness and applicability. Nevertheless, additional validation through real-world case studies or simulation-based experiments would further enhance the practical relevance and external validity of the approach as future research.

Validation: The model has been validated through illustrative examples, comparative assessments, and sensitivity analyses, which provide meaningful insights into its robustness and applicability. Nevertheless, additional validation through real-world case studies or simulation-based experiments would further enhance the practical relevance and external validity of the approach as future research.

Conclusions

In this article, an integrated problem of construction and production supply chain network has been considered, encompassing raw material suppliers, industrial production centers, distribution centers, customers, building material suppliers, contractors, design companies, and construction sites. In this problem, construction of facilities in the both production and retailer locations is considered. A proportional amount of constructions and designs are allocated to the contractors and design companies. Design companies are responsible for designing selected construction sites, preparing and providing design documents and drawings. The aim was to optimally decide about:

The production centers that should be opened and constructed (out of a list of potential sites).The distribution centers that should be opened and constructed (out of a list of potential sites).The contractors that should be selected and contracted with (out of a list of potential contractors).The design companies that should be selected and contracted with (out of a list of potential design companies).The suppliers of building materials that should be selected and contracted with (out of a list of potential suppliers).The amount of building materials transported from suppliers to contractors.The amount of construction area should be constructed by each contractor.The amount of design area should be designed by each design company.The suppliers of raw materials that should be selected and contracted with (out of a list of potential suppliers).The amount of raw materials transported from suppliers to production centers.The number of products that produced in production centers.The number of products transported from production centers to distribution centers.The number of products transported from distribution centers to customers.

The production centers that should be opened and constructed (out of a list of potential sites).

The production centers that should be opened and constructed (out of a list of potential sites).

The distribution centers that should be opened and constructed (out of a list of potential sites).

The distribution centers that should be opened and constructed (out of a list of potential sites).

The contractors that should be selected and contracted with (out of a list of potential contractors).

The contractors that should be selected and contracted with (out of a list of potential contractors).

The design companies that should be selected and contracted with (out of a list of potential design companies).

The design companies that should be selected and contracted with (out of a list of potential design companies).

The suppliers of building materials that should be selected and contracted with (out of a list of potential suppliers).

The suppliers of building materials that should be selected and contracted with (out of a list of potential suppliers).

The amount of building materials transported from suppliers to contractors.

The amount of building materials transported from suppliers to contractors.

The amount of construction area should be constructed by each contractor.

The amount of construction area should be constructed by each contractor.

The amount of design area should be designed by each design company.

The amount of design area should be designed by each design company.

The suppliers of raw materials that should be selected and contracted with (out of a list of potential suppliers).

The suppliers of raw materials that should be selected and contracted with (out of a list of potential suppliers).

The amount of raw materials transported from suppliers to production centers.

The amount of raw materials transported from suppliers to production centers.

The number of products that produced in production centers.

The number of products transported from distribution centers to customers.

The number of products transported from distribution centers to customers.

To this end, a mathematical programming approach is considered and a mixed integer linear programming optimization model is proposed with the objective of minimizing the total cost of supply chain, including 32 different costs. These costs can be summarized into following main categories:

The construction costs (for distribution and production centers).The design costs (for distribution and production centers).The contraction costs (with suppliers, contractors, and design companies).The production costs (for production centers).The transportation costs (for materials, products, workers).The land purchase costs (for distribution and production centers).The material purchase costs (for raw and building materials).The worker costs (salary).The holding/storing costs (for materials).

The construction costs (for distribution and production centers).

The construction costs (for distribution and production centers).

The design costs (for distribution and production centers).

The design costs (for distribution and production centers).

The contraction costs (with suppliers, contractors, and design companies).

The contraction costs (with suppliers, contractors, and design companies).

The production costs (for production centers).

The production costs (for production centers).

The transportation costs (for materials, products, workers).

The transportation costs (for materials, products, workers).

The land purchase costs (for distribution and production centers).

The land purchase costs (for distribution and production centers).

The material purchase costs (for raw and building materials).

The material purchase costs (for raw and building materials).

The worker costs (salary).

The worker costs (salary).

The holding/storing costs (for materials).

The holding/storing costs (for materials).

The proposed mathematical model has 18 decision variables and 66 technical constraints. A discussion is conducted based on three different parts: (i) an illustrative example, (ii) a comparative analysis, and (iii) a sensitivity analysis. At first part, an illustrative example was presented as a case problem with all details of input parameters and optimal output decision variables. In addition, the structure (topology) of obtained supply chain is depicted in this section, as well. In second part, we did several comprehensive comparative studies by using a wide range of test problems. We tried to consider a variety range of problems, and some charts were also provided to illustrate the increasing trend of dimensions for these test problems. The study used randomly generated test instances across small, medium, and large sized categories to investigate the methodology performance. These diverse problem instances allowed testing of the model’s scalability, robustness, and performance. This approach avoids reliance on a single example and ensures broader validation.

In the Illustrative Example section, we have considered multiple structure of products, each with different parameters such as demands, transportation costs, and resource needs. These variations result in different location and allocation decisions in the supply chain and reflects the effect of product characteristics on supply chain structure. Furthermore, in the Comparative Analysis section, we solved twelve additional test problems with different sizes (small, medium, and large), each one with diverse structures. Therefore, both the illustrative example and the comparative analysis explicitly incorporate product diversity, ensuring that the proposed model adequately considers the impact of product variations on supply chain design.

The optimal values and percentages of costs were reported for all of 32 types of costs separately. The results demonstrated that the most expensive parts of supply chain are related to:

TCPL: The cost of purchasing land for production centers construction.TCLP: The cost of purchasing land for distribution centers construction.TPEM: The total cost of equipment and machinery.TEW: The total cost of workers.

TCPL: The cost of purchasing land for production centers construction.

TCPL: The cost of purchasing land for production centers construction.

TCLP: The cost of purchasing land for distribution centers construction.

TCLP: The cost of purchasing land for distribution centers construction.

TPEM: The total cost of equipment and machinery.

TPEM: The total cost of equipment and machinery.

TEW: The total cost of workers.

TEW: The total cost of workers.

Besides, the least expensive parts of supply chain are related to:

TRSP: The total cost of transporting raw material from supplier to production center.TRPD: The total cost of transporting raw material from production center to wholesaler.TRDC: The total cost of transporting raw material from production center to retailers.TPP: The total cost of producing products at production center.

TRSP: The total cost of transporting raw material from supplier to production center.

TRSP: The total cost of transporting raw material from supplier to production center.

TRPD: The total cost of transporting raw material from production center to wholesaler.

TRPD: The total cost of transporting raw material from production center to wholesaler.

TRDC: The total cost of transporting raw material from production center to retailers.

TRDC: The total cost of transporting raw material from production center to retailers.

TPP: The total cost of producing products at production center.

TPP: The total cost of producing products at production center.

At third part of discussion section, we did a sensitivity analysis to assess the sensitivity of total cost in terms of input parameters. For this purpose, we summarized the major important input parameters into four following categories:

Fixed costs.Transportation costs.Demands.Land costs.

Fixed costs.

Fixed costs.

Transportation costs.

Transportation costs.

Demands.

Demands.

Land costs.

Land costs.

The results showed that the priority of parameters in terms of magnitude of impacts on total cost, in ascending order, was: (i) Fixed costs, (ii) Transportation costs, (iii) Land costs, and (iv) Demands.

As some directions for future research, the following suggestions can be considered by other researchers:

Considering risk management tools for addressing uncertainties in the problem.Incorporating project scheduling aspects such as time, cost, and quality tradeoffs into the problem.Incorporating transportation scheduling and vehicle routings into the problem.Considering a multi-objective optimization problem instead of single-objective one, by using other potential criteria (in addition to the total cost) such as environmental impact of supply chains, social aspects of supply chain, resiliency, and so on.Using simulation tools to validate the results of presented mathematical model.Extending the model for sustainability by considering social costs (e.g., labor welfare, community impacts) and environmental factors (e.g., carbon footprint, energy consumption, green construction practices).Incorporating dynamic cost parameters and multi-period planning in the model in order to address potential uncertainties.
